# Tzaddikim nominee expansion — 2026-09-30

Silas, 2026-09-30: at least 72 nominees for every tag section, researched for the best people, with
Keanu Reeves, Abraham Lincoln and Jimmy Carter named directly and a blacklist (`../blacklist.yaml`) for
problematic figures. Everyone below was promoted straight into the gallery seed set
(`../seed-sets.yaml`, kind_robots `config/tzaddik-initial-seeds.json`). Living/memorial status was
checked against Wikidata on 2026-09-30 and is refreshed again at import. Kept in `discovery/` so
`excluded_names()` never re-suggests these people.

## Living suggestions

### Sal Khan — United States — Free online education, Khan Academy
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Sal Khan began making short math videos to tutor his cousins and turned them into Khan Academy, a nonprofit offering free lessons to anyone with an internet connection. Its courses are used by millions of learners worldwide in dozens of languages.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sal_Khan

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Brewster Kahle — United States — Founder of the Internet Archive
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Journalism & Truth"]} -->
**Pitch:** Brewster Kahle founded the Internet Archive in 1996, whose Wayback Machine has preserved hundreds of billions of web pages. The archive also offers millions of free books, recordings and films as a public digital library.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brewster_Kahle

**Review note:** The Archive's lending of scanned books without publisher permission during 2020 was found to infringe copyright in Hachette v. Internet Archive.

### Ziauddin Yousafzai — Pakistan — Schoolteacher and girls' education advocate
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** Ziauddin Yousafzai founded and ran a school in Swat that taught girls and spoke out publicly when the Taliban banned girls' schooling there. He raised his daughter Malala to speak freely and continues to campaign for girls' education.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ziauddin_Yousafzai

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Sakena Yacoobi — Afghanistan — Founder of the Afghan Institute of Learning
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** Sakena Yacoobi founded the Afghan Institute of Learning in 1995 and ran underground home schools for thousands of girls while the Taliban banned girls' education. Her organisation has reached millions of Afghans with schooling and health training.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakena_Yacoobi

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Geoffrey Canada — United States — Harlem Children's Zone founder
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Community & Mutual Aid"]} -->
**Pitch:** Geoffrey Canada built the Harlem Children's Zone, which surrounds children in a 97-block area with schooling, health and family support from birth to college. It became a model for the federal Promise Neighborhoods programme.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Canada

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Erin Gruwell — United States — Teacher behind the Freedom Writers
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Erin Gruwell's Long Beach students, many written off as unteachable, wrote journals about their lives that became The Freedom Writers Diary. Her foundation trains teachers to use the same approach.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erin_Gruwell

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### LeVar Burton — United States — Actor and host of Reading Rainbow
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Pop Culture", "Arts & Culture"]} -->
**Pitch:** LeVar Burton hosted Reading Rainbow for more than two decades, encouraging generations of children to read. He continues to promote reading through his podcast LeVar Burton Reads.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LeVar_Burton

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Bill Nye — United States — Science educator and TV host
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Science & Medicine", "Pop Culture", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** Bill Nye's television show Bill Nye the Science Guy made hands-on science fun for a generation of children in the 1990s. He now leads The Planetary Society and speaks up for science literacy.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Nye

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Hank Green — United States — Educational YouTube creator
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Pop Culture", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Hank Green co-created Crash Course and SciShow, free video series used in classrooms around the world. With his brother John he also co-founded VidCon and the charity project Project for Awesome.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hank_Green

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### John Green — United States — Author and Crash Course co-creator
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Humanitarian", "Pop Culture", "Arts & Culture"]} -->
**Pitch:** Author John Green co-created the free Crash Course video lessons with his brother Hank. He has since campaigned for cheaper tuberculosis testing and treatment and backs maternal health care in Sierra Leone.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Green

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Sonam Wangchuk — India (Ladakh) — Education reformer and engineer
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** Sonam Wangchuk founded SECMOL to reform schooling in Ladakh, where most students had been failing state exams taught in unfamiliar languages. He also invented the ice stupa for storing winter water and campaigns for the fragile Himalayan environment.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonam_Wangchuk

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Bunker Roy — India — Founder of Barefoot College
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Community & Mutual Aid", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** Bunker Roy founded the Barefoot College in Tilonia, Rajasthan, which trains rural people, including grandmothers without formal schooling, as solar engineers and teachers. The model has spread to villages in dozens of countries.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunker_Roy

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Sugata Mitra — India — Researcher behind Hole in the Wall
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Sugata Mitra's Hole in the Wall experiment put computers in Delhi slum walls and found children taught themselves to use them. The work won the 2013 TED Prize and inspired self-organised learning projects.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugata_Mitra

**Review note:** Researchers have questioned how far his self-organised learning results generalise.

### Ranjitsinh Disale — India — Global Teacher Prize 2020 winner
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Ranjitsinh Disale added QR codes to textbooks in his village school in Maharashtra to link students to lessons in their own language, and the idea was adopted statewide. He won the 2020 Global Teacher Prize and shared half the prize money with the other finalists.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranjitsinh_Disale

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Nancie Atwell — United States — Reading and writing teacher
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Nancie Atwell founded the Center for Teaching and Learning in Maine, a demonstration school where students read dozens of books a year. She won the first Global Teacher Prize in 2015 and gave the prize money to her school.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancie_Atwell

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Hanan Al Hroub — Palestine — Teacher, play-based learning
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->
**Pitch:** Hanan Al Hroub, who grew up in a refugee camp, developed play-based teaching to help children traumatised by violence feel safe in school. She won the 2016 Global Teacher Prize.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanan_Al_Hroub

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Maggie MacDonnell — Canada — Teacher in an Inuit community
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Community & Mutual Aid"]} -->
**Pitch:** Maggie MacDonnell taught in Salluit, a fly-in Inuit village in Nunavik troubled by youth suicide, running life-skills and fitness programmes that raised graduation rates. She won the 2017 Global Teacher Prize.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maggie_MacDonnell

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Andria Zafirakou — United Kingdom — Art teacher, Global Teacher Prize
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Arts & Culture"]} -->
**Pitch:** Andria Zafirakou learned basics of many of her London students' languages and redesigned the timetable to keep at-risk children in school. She won the 2018 Global Teacher Prize and founded Artists in Residence.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andria_Zafirakou

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Kakenya Ntaiya — Kenya — Founder of a girls' boarding school
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice", "Community & Mutual Aid"]} -->
**Pitch:** Kakenya Ntaiya bargained with her Maasai village elders to be allowed to go to college, then returned with a doctorate to open the Kakenya Center for Excellence, a girls' school. Its students commit to avoiding FGM and early marriage.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakenya_Ntaiya

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Ann Cotton — United Kingdom — Founder of Camfed
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Humanitarian"]} -->
**Pitch:** Ann Cotton founded Camfed in 1993 after learning that poverty, not culture, kept Zimbabwean girls out of school. Camfed has supported millions of girls through school in sub-Saharan Africa.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Cotton

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Ruby Bridges — United States — First Black child in an all-white New Orleans school
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue", "Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** At six years old, Ruby Bridges walked past screaming crowds with federal marshals to integrate William Frantz Elementary School in 1960. She now runs a foundation promoting tolerance through education.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_Bridges

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Elizabeth Eckford — United States — Member of the Little Rock Nine
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue", "Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Elizabeth Eckford faced a mob alone when she tried to enter Little Rock Central High School in 1957, in an image seen around the world. She and the other eight students integrated the school under federal protection.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Eckford

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Tererai Trent — Zimbabwe — Education advocate who built schools
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Tererai Trent, married young in rural Zimbabwe, buried her written dreams under a rock and went on to earn a doctorate in the United States. She returned to build and rebuild schools in her home community.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tererai_Trent

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** Pashtana Durrani founded LEARN Afghanistan, which runs digital learning and teaches girls in secret since the Taliban banned them from secondary school. She speaks publicly for Afghan girls' right to learn.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Matiullah Wesa — Afghanistan — Campaigner for girls' schooling
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** Matiullah Wesa's PenPath group ran mobile libraries and campaigned village to village for schools in rural Afghanistan. He was detained by the Taliban in 2023 for advocating girls' education.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Aziz Royesh co-founded the Marefat School in Kabul, which educated thousands of girls and boys from the Hazara community. He was a top-ten finalist for the 2016 Global Teacher Prize.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue", "Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** After surviving a gang rape ordered by a village council in 2002, Mukhtaran Mai refused to stay silent and took her attackers to court. She used compensation money to open schools for girls in her village.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mukhtar_Mai

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Community & Mutual Aid"]} -->
**Pitch:** As a teenager Humaira Bachal began teaching other children in her Karachi neighbourhood, which grew into the Dream Model Street School serving over a thousand students. She has faced threats for educating girls.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humaira_Bachal

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Mitchel Resnick's MIT group created Scratch, a free block-based programming language used by tens of millions of children. He promotes playful, creative learning for all kids.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Guatemalan computer scientist Luis von Ahn co-founded Duolingo to make language learning free for people who cannot afford classes. He also invented CAPTCHA and reCAPTCHA, which helped digitise books.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Jonathan Kozol's books, from Death at an Early Age to Savage Inequalities, documented how poor and minority children are failed by underfunded schools. He has spent decades advocating for school equity.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Kozol

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Carla Hayden — United States — 14th Librarian of Congress
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Carla Hayden became the first woman and first African American to serve as Librarian of Congress in 2016 and worked to open its collections to the public online. Earlier, she kept Baltimore's libraries open during the 2015 unrest.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carla_Hayden

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Madhav Chavan co-founded Pratham in 1995 to bring education to children in Mumbai's slums, and it grew into one of India's largest education NGOs. He shared the WISE Prize for Education in 2012.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhav_Chavan

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Safeena Husain — India — Founder of Educate Girls
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Community & Mutual Aid"]} -->
**Pitch:** Safeena Husain founded Educate Girls, which works with village volunteers to enrol out-of-school girls in rural India. The organisation received the Ramon Magsaysay Award in 2025.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safeena_Husain

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Marian Wright Edelman — United States — Founder of the Children's Defense Fund
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice", "Community & Mutual Aid"]} -->
**Pitch:** Marian Wright Edelman, the first Black woman admitted to the Mississippi bar, founded the Children's Defense Fund in 1973. It has fought for children's health, Head Start and education for decades.

### Freeman A. Hrabowski III — United States — President of UMBC
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Freeman Hrabowski, jailed as a child marcher in Birmingham in 1963, led the University of Maryland, Baltimore County for three decades. His Meyerhoff Scholars programme became a national model for Black students in science.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Zhang Guimei — China — Founder of a free girls' high school
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Zhang Guimei founded Huaping High School for Girls in Yunnan in 2008, China's first free public high school for girls. Nearly all its graduates have gone to university.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Guimei

### Jane Elliott — United States — Anti-racism educator
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** The day after Martin Luther King Jr. was killed, Iowa teacher Jane Elliott ran her 'blue eyes/brown eyes' exercise to teach her pupils about discrimination. She has taught it to audiences ever since.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jane_Elliott

**Review note:** Some researchers question the ethics of the exercise's use of humiliation.

### Tilly Smith — United Kingdom — Schoolgirl who warned of a tsunami
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue", "Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Ten-year-old Tilly Smith recognised signs of a tsunami from a geography lesson on a Thai beach in 2004 and warned her parents, who helped clear the beach. Around a hundred people are credited as saved.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tilly_Smith

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Wesley Autrey — United States — Subway rescuer
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 2007 construction worker Wesley Autrey jumped onto New York subway tracks and pinned a man having a seizure between the rails as a train passed over them. Both survived.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wesley_Autrey

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** Captain Chesley Sullenberger landed US Airways Flight 1549 on the Hudson River in 2009 after both engines failed, and all 155 people aboard survived. He has since advocated for aviation safety.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sully_Sullenberger

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 2018 Malian migrant Mamoudou Gassama climbed four storeys of a Paris building to rescue a child dangling from a balcony. He was granted French citizenship and offered a place with the Paris fire brigade.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamoudou_Gassama

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Rick Stanton — United Kingdom — Cave diver in the Thai cave rescue
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** British cave diver Rick Stanton was among the first to reach the trapped Thai youth football team in 2018 and helped bring them out. He has taken part in many cave rescues.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Stanton

### John Volanthen — United Kingdom — Cave diver in the Thai cave rescue
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** John Volanthen found the trapped Wild Boars football team in Tham Luang cave in 2018 and helped carry them out underwater. He also works with Scouts.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Volanthen

**Pitch:** US Army reservist Joseph Darby turned over photos of prisoner abuse at Abu Ghraib to investigators in 2004. He received threats after being publicly identified.

**Pitch:** Emergency department director Ai Fen circulated a lab report about the new coronavirus in December 2019 and was reprimanded. She later spoke out publicly.

She returned to Lesbos to volunteer with a search-and-rescue group and was arrested by Greek authorities in 2018 on charges widely condemned by human rights groups.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** After the 2019 Christchurch mosque shootings she led with compassion toward Muslim New Zealanders and passed a ban on military-style semi-automatic weapons within weeks. She also launched the Christchurch Call against online terrorist content and put wellbeing measures at the centre of her government's budgets.

She shared the 2011 Nobel Peace Prize for her non-violent work for women's safety and rights, and she received the Ibrahim Prize for her peaceful handover of power.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellen_Johnson_Sirleaf

**Review note:** She gave early financial support to Charles Taylor, which led Liberia's Truth and Reconciliation Commission to recommend she be barred from office, and she was criticised for appointing her sons to senior posts.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** As president of Cape Verde from 2001 to 2011 he helped consolidate one of Africa's most stable democracies and stepped down at the end of his term limit. His record won him the 2011 Ibrahim Prize for Achievement in African Leadership.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Pires

**Review note:** He had earlier served as prime minister in Cape Verde's one-party state from 1975 to 1991.

### Abdou Diouf — Senegal — President of Senegal who conceded defeat
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** After two decades as president he conceded defeat in Senegal's 2000 election and handed power to Abdoulaye Wade, a rare peaceful transfer in the region at the time. He then served as Secretary General of the Francophonie.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdou_Diouf

**Review note:** His long rule saw little progress in ending the Casamance conflict.

### Michelle Bachelet — Chile — President of Chile, UN human rights chief
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** A survivor of detention and torture under Pinochet, she became Chile's first woman president and expanded pensions, early education and reproductive rights. She was also the first head of UN Women and later served as UN High Commissioner for Human Rights.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Bachelet

**Review note:** Human rights groups criticised her 2022 visit to Xinjiang as too soft on China.

### Tarja Halonen — Finland — First woman president of Finland
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** She served as Finland's first woman president from 2000 to 2012 after a long career advocating for human rights, sexual minorities and gender equality. She co-chaired the UN High-level Panel on Global Sustainability.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Environment", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Norway's first woman prime minister, she chaired the commission whose 1987 report 'Our Common Future' defined sustainable development. As Director-General of the WHO she led the global tobacco-control treaty.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Arts & Culture"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 1980 Icelanders made her the world's first democratically elected woman president, a year after she was a single mother known for her work in theatre. Over sixteen years in office she championed the Icelandic language, reforestation and girls' ambitions.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigd%C3%ADs_Finnbogad%C3%B3ttir

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 2009 she became the world's first openly lesbian head of government, leading Iceland after its banking collapse. Her government legalised same-sex marriage in 2010, and she married her partner under the new law.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B3hanna_Sigur%C3%B0ard%C3%B3ttir

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Angela Merkel — Germany — German chancellor for sixteen years
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Humanitarian"]} -->
**Pitch:** A physicist from East Germany, she led Germany steadily for sixteen years through financial, euro and pandemic crises. In 2015 she kept the borders open to more than a million refugees under the motto 'Wir schaffen das' ('We can do this').

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel

**Review note:** Critics fault her for deepening Germany's dependence on Russian gas through Nord Stream 2 and for pressing harsh austerity on Greece.

### Mary McAleese — Ireland — President of Ireland, bridge-builder
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->
**Pitch:** As President of Ireland from 1997 to 2011, with 'building bridges' as her theme, she reached out to unionist communities in Northern Ireland and hosted Queen Elizabeth II's historic 2011 state visit. She is an outspoken advocate for LGBT people within the Catholic Church.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_McAleese

**Review note:** In 2005 she apologised for remarks comparing the teaching of hatred toward Catholics in Northern Ireland to Nazi-era hatred of Jews.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Peace & Diplomacy", "Politics"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** After the 2010 uprising she led Kyrgyzstan's interim government, organised a new parliamentary constitution, and stepped down in 2011 as she had promised. That made her the first woman head of state in Central Asia and a rare voluntary transfer of power in the region.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Environment"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->

### James Hansen — United States — Climate scientist

### Michael E. Mann — United States — Climate scientist
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Environment", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Mann's 1998-99 'hockey stick' reconstruction showed that recent warming is unusual in at least a thousand years, a finding later confirmed by many independent studies. He has defended climate science through years of political attacks and harassment.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_E._Mann

### Syukuro Manabe — Japan / United States — Climate modeller
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Environment", "Science & Medicine"]} -->

### Kris Tompkins — United States / Chile — Conservationist, national parks
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "living", "tags": ["Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** With her late husband Douglas, Tompkins bought and restored vast areas of Patagonia and then gave them away, including a roughly one-million-acre donation to Chile in 2018 that helped create five new national parks. ## Memorial suggestions

### Savitribai Phule — India — Girls' education and caste reform
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Savitribai Phule co-founded one of the first schools for girls in India in Pune in 1848 and taught there despite harassment from people who opposed educating girls and lower-caste children. She also ran a shelter for widows and died in 1897 after caring for plague patients.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savitribai_Phule

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Jyotirao Phule — India — Anti-caste reformer and school founder
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Jyotirao Phule opened schools for girls and for Dalit children in 1840s Pune, working alongside his wife Savitribai. He founded the Satyashodhak Samaj to challenge caste discrimination and wrote widely against untouchability.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jyotirao_Phule

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Maria Montessori — Italy — Physician and child-centred education pioneer
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Education", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Maria Montessori, one of Italy's first women physicians, opened her Casa dei Bambini in a poor Rome neighbourhood in 1907 and developed a method built on children's self-directed learning. Montessori schools now operate on every inhabited continent.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Montessori

**Review note:** She cooperated with Mussolini's government for a period in the 1920s before breaking with it and leaving Italy in 1934.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Paulo Freire's literacy campaigns taught Brazilian farm workers to read in weeks by building lessons from their own lives, work that got him jailed and exiled after the 1964 coup. His book Pedagogy of the Oppressed became one of the most cited works in education.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Anne Sullivan — United States — Teacher of Helen Keller
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** Anne Sullivan, herself partially blind and raised in a poorhouse, became Helen Keller's teacher in 1887 and broke through by spelling words into her hand. She remained Keller's companion and teacher for nearly fifty years.

### Mary McLeod Bethune — United States — Educator and civil rights leader
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Education", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Mary McLeod Bethune founded a school for Black girls in Daytona Beach in 1904 with a few dollars, which grew into Bethune-Cookman University. She later advised Franklin Roosevelt and founded the National Council of Negro Women.

He helped defeat the Briggs Initiative's attempt to fire gay teachers before he was assassinated in 1978, and his call for people to come out still inspires LGBTQ people.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvey_Milk

**Review note:** Biographer Randy Shilts documented that, years before his political career, Milk began a relationship with Jack McKinley when McKinley was 16; the account is a real and recurring objection to his legacy.

**Review note:** The 1973 IB affair revealed a secret intelligence bureau with ties to his Social Democrats that had spied on left-wing Swedes.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->

He returned to champion equal rights for freed people and pressed for the civil rights bill that passed as the Civil Rights Act of 1875.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** As US Secretary of Labor from 1933 to 1945, the first woman to serve in a US cabinet, she drove Social Security, the minimum wage and the forty-hour week. She came into public life after witnessing the 1911 Triangle Shirtwaist Factory fire.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 1968 she became the first Black woman elected to the US Congress, and in 1972 she became the first Black candidate to seek a major party's presidential nomination. 'Unbought and unbossed', she fought for school meals, the poor and women's rights.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirley_Chisholm

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Barbara Jordan — United States — Texas congresswoman and constitutional voice
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** She was the first Black woman elected to Congress from the South, and her 1974 statement during the Watergate impeachment hearings became a classic defence of the Constitution. In Congress she worked to extend the Voting Rights Act to language minorities.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Jordan

**Review note:** As chair of the US Commission on Immigration Reform she recommended lowering legal immigration levels, a stance restrictionists later cited.

### Margaret Chase Smith — United States — US senator who confronted McCarthy
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** The first woman to serve in both houses of Congress, she delivered her 1950 'Declaration of Conscience' against McCarthyism at a time when few colleagues dared to. She served Maine for more than three decades and was known for her independence.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Jeannette Rankin — United States — First woman elected to US Congress
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->
**Pitch:** Elected in 1916, four years before national women's suffrage, she was the first woman in Congress. She voted against US entry into both world wars and was the only member to vote against war after Pearl Harbor, and she was still leading anti-war marches in 1968.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 1989 this Solidarity adviser became the first non-communist prime minister in the Soviet bloc, steering Poland's peaceful transition to democracy. As UN Special Rapporteur on the former Yugoslavia, he resigned in 1995 in protest at the world's failure to prevent the Srebrenica massacre.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Kim Dae-jung — South Korea — South Korean president, Sunshine Policy
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Peace & Diplomacy", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** He survived a 1973 kidnapping and a 1980 death sentence under military rule, and he became South Korea's president in 1998. His Sunshine Policy produced the first inter-Korean summit in 2000 and won him the Nobel Peace Prize.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Dae-jung

**Review note:** It later emerged that Hyundai had made secret payments to North Korea ahead of the 2000 summit.

### Corazon Aquino — Philippines — President after the People Power Revolution
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics"]} -->
**Pitch:** After her husband's assassination she led the 1986 People Power Revolution that peacefully ousted Ferdinand Marcos. As president she restored democratic institutions under the 1987 constitution, and in 1992 she handed over power rather than seek another term.

**Review note:** Troops killed protesting farmers in the 1987 Mendiola massacre under her government, and her land reform let her family's Hacienda Luisita avoid redistribution.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->
**Pitch:** As Botswana's president from 1980 to 1998 he sustained its democracy and its prudent use of diamond revenue, and he stepped down voluntarily. He later facilitated the Inter-Congolese Dialogue and chaired the international panel on the Rwandan genocide.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Politics", "Science & Medicine"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice", "Humanitarian"]} -->
**Pitch:** The first Black man to win Wimbledon was also an outspoken opponent of apartheid who was arrested protesting outside the South African embassy in 1985. After contracting HIV from a blood transfusion, he spent his final year fighting stigma and founded a foundation to defeat AIDS.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Ashe

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Arts & Culture", "Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** He created the Muppets and helped shape Sesame Street, which has taught letters, numbers and kindness to children worldwide since 1969. His characters modelled warmth, inclusion and silliness for generations of families.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Terry Pratchett — United Kingdom — Discworld novelist and dementia advocate
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Arts & Culture", "Pop Culture", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** His Discworld novels used comedy to skewer prejudice, cruelty and power, and remain loved by millions of readers. After his early-onset Alzheimer's diagnosis he donated a million dollars to research and campaigned openly for dementia funding and the right to die with dignity.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Pratchett

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Bob Ross — United States — Painter and television art teacher
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Arts & Culture", "Education"]} -->
**Pitch:** On The Joy of Painting from 1983 to 1994 he taught millions of viewers to paint landscapes in half an hour, insisting there are no mistakes, only happy accidents. His gentleness and encouragement made art feel possible for people who had never picked up a brush.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Ross

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Arts & Culture", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** His black-and-white photographs of labourers, migrants and famine gave dignity and visibility to people often ignored by the world. With his wife Lélia Wanick Salgado he founded Instituto Terra, which replanted millions of trees on degraded land in Brazil's Atlantic Forest.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A3o_Salgado

**Review note:** Some critics argued his images aestheticised suffering, turning poverty into beautiful pictures for gallery audiences.

### Elizabeth Taylor — United Kingdom / United States — Film star and AIDS activist
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Humanitarian", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** At a time when few celebrities would say the word AIDS, she co-founded amfAR in 1985 and later her own AIDS foundation. She lobbied governments and raised large sums for research and care when people with HIV were being shunned.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Taylor

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### Muhammad Ali — United States — Boxing champion and conscientious objector
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->
**Pitch:** He gave up his title and years of his prime rather than fight in Vietnam, and his conviction was overturned by the Supreme Court in 1971. He later became a global humanitarian figure and a symbol of courage in living with Parkinson's disease.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali

**Review note:** In his Nation of Islam years he voiced support for racial separatism, and his cruel taunting of Joe Frazier drew lasting criticism.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice", "Courage & Rescue"]} -->
**Pitch:** In 1947 he broke Major League Baseball's colour line, enduring abuse with extraordinary restraint and opening the sport to Black players. He went on to work for civil rights causes and business development in Black communities.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Robinson

**Review note:** He testified before the House Un-American Activities Committee in 1949 against Paul Robeson's statements, which he later said he regretted.

### Bill Russell — United States — Basketball champion and civil rights activist
<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** The winner of 11 NBA titles was also the league's first Black head coach and marched with Martin Luther King Jr. He spoke out against racism throughout his career, and received the Presidential Medal of Freedom in 2011.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Russell

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Arts & Culture", "Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** A celebrated bass-baritone and actor, he used his fame to campaign against lynching, segregation and colonialism. He paid for it with a revoked passport and a blacklisted career during the McCarthy era.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Robeson

**Review note:** He defended Stalin's Soviet Union and did not publicly condemn its purges.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Humanitarian"]} -->
**Pitch:** After his death it became clear he had given millions privately, including to Childline, AIDS charities and individuals in need. He also stood as a gay icon who refused to be shamed.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Arts & Culture", "Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->
**Pitch:** Known as Mama Africa, she carried South African music to the world while exiled for 31 years. She testified against apartheid at the United Nations.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Arts & Culture", "Peace & Diplomacy"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Pop Culture", "Civil Rights & Justice", "Courage & Rescue"]} -->

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Science & Medicine", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** Patterson used lead isotopes to calculate the age of the Earth at about 4.55 billion years, and in the process discovered that industrial lead from petrol was contaminating the whole planet. <!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Science & Medicine", "Environment"]} -->

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Needleman

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Science & Medicine", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** With Sherwood Rowland, Molina showed in 1974 that CFCs from aerosols and refrigerants destroy the ozone layer. Their warning led to the Montreal Protocol, the most successful environmental treaty so far, and a shared 1995 Nobel Prize in Chemistry.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

### F. Sherwood Rowland — United States — Chemist, ozone depletion

**Pitch:** Rowland co-discovered with Mario Molina that CFCs destroy stratospheric ozone, and spent years pressing the case publicly despite fierce industry pushback. Their work led to the Montreal Protocol and a shared 1995 Nobel Prize.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Science & Medicine", "Environment"]} -->
**Pitch:** Farman and his British Antarctic Survey colleagues reported the Antarctic ozone hole in 1985, using decades of patient ground measurements. The shock of their discovery helped secure the 1987 Montreal Protocol phasing out CFCs.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Environment", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Keeling began precise measurements of atmospheric carbon dioxide at Mauna Loa, Hawaii, in 1958. The resulting Keeling Curve, showing CO2 rising year after year, became the foundational evidence of human-driven greenhouse gas build-up.

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Science & Medicine", "Environment", "Civil Rights & Justice"]} -->

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eunice_Newton_Foote

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Environment", "Science & Medicine"]} -->

**Review note:** No significant documented controversy found.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Environment", "Science & Medicine", "Journalism & Truth"]} -->
**Pitch:** Carson's 1962 book Silent Spring exposed the dangers of indiscriminate pesticide use. It helped launch the modern environmental movement and the US ban on DDT for agriculture.

**Wikipedia:** https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Carson

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Environment", "Science & Medicine"]} -->
**Pitch:** Leopold pushed for the Gila Wilderness, designated in 1924 as the world's first administratively protected wilderness area. His book A Sand County Almanac set out a 'land ethic' that treats people as members of the land community, shaping modern conservation.

<!-- tzaddik-meta: {"status": "deceased", "tags": ["Environment", "Politics"]} -->
